The mplot3d toolkit                      *matplotlib-explain-toolkits-mplot3d*

*explain-toolkits-mplot3d-mplot3d*

Generating 3D plots using the mplot3d toolkit.

This tutorial showcases various 3D plots. Click on the figures to see each
full gallery example with the code that generates the figures.

3D Axes (of class
|toolkits-mplot3d-axes3d-mpl_toolkits.mplot3d.axes3d.Axes3D|) are created by
passing the `projection="3d"` keyword argument to
|matplotlib.figure.Figure.add_subplot|:

>python
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
    fig = plt.figure()
    ax = fig.add_subplot(projection='3d')
<
Multiple 3D subplots can be added on the same figure, as for 2D subplots.

  Changed in version 3.2.0: ~

  |api-toolkits-mplot3d-module-mpl_toolkits.mplot3d| module to make the '3d'
  projection to |matplotlib.figure.Figure.add_subplot|.

See the |toolkits-mplot3d-faq-toolkit-mplot3d-faq| for more information
about the mplot3d toolkit.

Frequently Asked Questions                              *matplotlib-users-faq*

*users-faq-howto-faq*

I DON'T SEE A FIGURE WINDOW            *users-faq-i-don-t-see-a-figure-window*

*users-faq-how-do-no-figure*

Please see |explain-figure-backends-figures-not-showing|.

      *users-faq-why-do-i-have-so-many-ticks-and-or-why-are-they-out-of-order*
WHY DO I HAVE SO MANY TICKS, AND/OR WHY ARE THEY OUT OF ORDER?

*users-faq-how-to-too-many-ticks*

One common cause for unexpected tick behavior is passing a list of strings
instead of numbers or datetime objects. This can easily happen without
notice when reading in a comma-delimited text file. Matplotlib treats lists
of strings as categorical variables (Plotting categorical variables
https://matplotlib.org/stable/gallery/lines_bars_and_markers/categorical_variables.html),
and by default puts one tick per category, and plots them in the order in
which they are supplied.

>python
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
    import numpy as np

    fig, ax = plt.subplots(1, 2, layout='constrained', figsize=(6, 2))

    ax[0].set_title('Ticks seem out of order / misplaced')
    x = ['5', '20', '1', '9']  # strings
    y = [5, 20, 1, 9]
    ax[0].plot(x, y, 'd')
    ax[0].tick_params(axis='x', labelcolor='red', labelsize=14)

    ax[1].set_title('Many ticks')
    x = [str(xx) for xx in np.arange(100)]  # strings
    y = np.arange(100)
    ax[1].plot(x, y)
    ax[1].tick_params(axis='x', labelcolor='red', labelsize=14)
<
(`Source code`
https://matplotlib.org/stable/_downloads/5a1bd6b33d2fad4b4fde85b7d0324aa9/faq-1.py,
`2x.png`
https://matplotlib.org/stable/_downloads/156cbc0e2a724bcbf8ea4dfbca668dcd/faq-1.2x.png,
`png`
https://matplotlib.org/stable/_downloads/e7ff1e5d25c51f435350824e98cb7d73/faq-1.png)

The solution is to convert the list of strings to numbers or datetime
objects (often `np.asarray(numeric_strings, dtype='float')` or
`np.asarray(datetime_strings, dtype='datetime64[s]')`).

For more information see Fixing too many ticks
https://matplotlib.org/stable/gallery/ticks/ticks_too_many.html.

                     *users-faq-determine-the-extent-of-artists-in-the-figure*
DETERMINE THE EXTENT OF ARTISTS IN THE FIGURE

*users-faq-howto-determine-artist-extent*

Sometimes we want to know the extent of an Artist. Matplotlib
|matplotlib.artist.Artist| objects have a method
|matplotlib.artist.Artist.get_window_extent| that will usually return the
extent of the artist in pixels. However, some artists, in particular text,
must be rendered at least once before their extent is known. Matplotlib
supplies |matplotlib.figure.Figure.draw_without_rendering|, which should be
called before calling `get_window_extent`.

CHECK WHETHER A FIGURE IS EMPTY    *users-faq-check-whether-a-figure-is-empty*

*users-faq-howto-figure-empty*

Empty can actually mean different things. Does the figure contain any
artists? Does a figure with an empty |matplotlib.axes.Axes| still count as
empty? Is the figure empty if it was rendered pure white (there may be
artists present, but they could be outside the drawing area or transparent)?

For the purpose here, we define empty as: "The figure does not contain any
artists except its background patch." The exception for the background is
necessary, because by default every figure contains a
|matplotlib.patches.Rectangle| as its background patch. This definition
could be checked via:

>python
    def is_empty(figure):
        """
        Return whether the figure contains no Artists (other than the default
        background patch).
        """
        contained_artists = figure.get_children()
        return len(contained_artists) <= 1
<
We've decided not to include this as a figure method because this is only
one way of defining empty, and checking the above is only rarely necessary.
Whether or not something has been added to the figure is usually defined
within the context of the program.

The only reliable way to check whether a figure would render empty is to
actually perform such a rendering and inspect the result.

                    *users-faq-find-all-objects-in-a-figure-of-a-certain-type*
FIND ALL OBJECTS IN A FIGURE OF A CERTAIN TYPE

*users-faq-howto-findobj*

Every Matplotlib artist (see |tutorials-artists-artists-tutorial|) has a
method called |matplotlib.artist.Artist.findobj| that can be used to
recursively search the artist for any artists it may contain that meet some
criteria (e.g., match all |matplotlib.lines.Line2D| instances or match some
arbitrary filter function). For example, the following snippet finds every
object in the figure which has a `set_color` property and makes the object
blue:

>python
    def myfunc(x):
        return hasattr(x, 'set_color')

    for o in fig.findobj(myfunc):
        o.set_color('blue')
<
You can also filter on class instances:

>python
    import matplotlib.text as text
    for o in fig.findobj(text.Text):
        o.set_fontstyle('italic')
<

                          *users-faq-prevent-ticklabels-from-having-an-offset*
PREVENT TICKLABELS FROM HAVING AN OFFSET

*users-faq-howto-suppress-offset*

The default formatter will use an offset to reduce the length of the
ticklabels. To turn this feature off on a per-axis basis:

>python
    ax.xaxis.get_major_formatter().set_useOffset(False)
<
set `rcParams["axes.formatter.useoffset"]` (default: `True`), or use a
different formatter. See |api-ticker_api-module-matplotlib.ticker| for
details.

SAVE TRANSPARENT FIGURES                  *users-faq-save-transparent-figures*

*users-faq-howto-transparent*

The |matplotlib.pyplot.savefig| command has a keyword argument transparent
which, if 'True', will make the figure and axes backgrounds transparent when
saving, but will not affect the displayed image on the screen.

If you need finer grained control, e.g., you do not want full transparency
or you want to affect the screen displayed version as well, you can set the
alpha properties directly. The figure has a |matplotlib.patches.Rectangle|
instance called patch and the axes has a Rectangle instance called patch.
You can set any property on them directly (facecolor, edgecolor, linewidth,
linestyle, alpha). e.g.:

>python
    fig = plt.figure()
    fig.patch.set_alpha(0.5)
    ax = fig.add_subplot(111)
    ax.patch.set_alpha(0.5)
<
If you need all the figure elements to be transparent, there is currently no
global alpha setting, but you can set the alpha channel on individual
elements, e.g.:

>python
    ax.plot(x, y, alpha=0.5)
    ax.set_xlabel('volts', alpha=0.5)
<

                               *users-faq-save-multiple-plots-to-one-pdf-file*
SAVE MULTIPLE PLOTS TO ONE PDF FILE

*users-faq-howto-multipage*

Many image file formats can only have one image per file, but some formats
support multi-page files. Currently, Matplotlib only provides multi-page
output to pdf files, using either the pdf or pgf backends, via the
|matplotlib.backends.backend_pdf.PdfPages| and `backend_pgf.PdfPages`
https://matplotlib.org/stable/api/backend_pgf_api.html#matplotlib.backends.backend_pgf.PdfPages
classes.

MAKE ROOM FOR TICK LABELS                *users-faq-make-room-for-tick-labels*

*users-faq-howto-auto-adjust*

By default, Matplotlib uses fixed percentage margins around subplots. This
can lead to labels overlapping or being cut off at the figure boundary.
There are multiple ways to fix this:

- Manually adapt the subplot parameters using
  |matplotlib.figure.Figure.subplots_adjust| /
  |matplotlib.pyplot.subplots_adjust|.
- Use one of the automatic layout mechanisms:

  - constrained layout
    (|explain-axes-constrainedlayout_guide-constrainedlayout-guide|)
  - tight layout (|explain-axes-tight_layout_guide-tight-layout-guide|)

- Calculate good values from the size of the plot elements yourself
  (Programmatically control subplot adjustment
  https://matplotlib.org/stable/gallery/subplots_axes_and_figures/auto_subplots_adjust.html)

                         *users-faq-align-my-ylabels-across-multiple-subplots*
ALIGN MY YLABELS ACROSS MULTIPLE SUBPLOTS

*users-faq-howto-align-label*

If you have multiple subplots over one another, and the y data have
different scales, you can often get ylabels that do not align vertically
across the multiple subplots, which can be unattractive. By default,
Matplotlib positions the x location of the ylabel so that it does not
overlap any of the y ticks. You can override this default behavior by
specifying the coordinates of the label. To learn how, see
Align labels and titles
https://matplotlib.org/stable/gallery/subplots_axes_and_figures/align_labels_demo.html

                           *users-faq-control-the-draw-order-of-plot-elements*
CONTROL THE DRAW ORDER OF PLOT ELEMENTS

*users-faq-howto-set-zorder*

The draw order of plot elements, and thus which elements will be on top, is
determined by the |matplotlib.artist.Artist.set_zorder| property. See
Zorder Demo https://matplotlib.org/stable/gallery/misc/zorder_demo.html for
a detailed description.

                             *users-faq-make-the-aspect-ratio-for-plots-equal*
MAKE THE ASPECT RATIO FOR PLOTS EQUAL

*users-faq-howto-axis-equal*

The Axes property |matplotlib.axes.Axes.set_aspect| controls the aspect
ratio of the axes. You can set it to be 'auto', 'equal', or some ratio which
controls the ratio:

>python
    ax = fig.add_subplot(111, aspect='equal')
<
See Equal axis aspect ratio
https://matplotlib.org/stable/gallery/subplots_axes_and_figures/axis_equal_demo.html
for a complete example.

DRAW MULTIPLE Y-AXIS SCALES            *users-faq-draw-multiple-y-axis-scales*

*users-faq-howto-twoscale*

A frequent request is to have two scales for the left and right y-axis,
which is possible using |matplotlib.pyplot.twinx| (more than two scales are
not currently supported, though it is on the wish list). This works pretty
well, though there are some quirks when you are trying to interactively pan
and zoom, because both scales do not get the signals.

The approach uses |matplotlib.pyplot.twinx| (and its sister
|matplotlib.pyplot.twiny|) to use 2 different axes, turning the axes
rectangular frame off on the 2nd axes to keep it from obscuring the first,
and manually setting the tick locs and labels as desired. You can use
separate `matplotlib.ticker` formatters and locators as desired because the
two axes are independent.

(`Source code`
https://matplotlib.org/stable/_downloads/44b5432bcc59ab8b9b8a6545b0ae281f/faq-2.py,
`2x.png`
https://matplotlib.org/stable/_downloads/5b35eae77b9d97177298700c52e5d640/faq-2.2x.png,
`png`
https://matplotlib.org/stable/_downloads/f55704b6a74aaf141da69633eba9787e/faq-2.png)

See Plots with different scales
https://matplotlib.org/stable/gallery/subplots_axes_and_figures/two_scales.html
for a complete example.

                    *users-faq-generate-images-without-having-a-window-appear*
GENERATE IMAGES WITHOUT HAVING A WINDOW APPEAR

*users-faq-howto-batch*

The recommended approach since Matplotlib 3.1 is to explicitly create a
Figure instance:

>python
    from matplotlib.figure import Figure
    fig = Figure()
    ax = fig.subplots()
    ax.plot([1, 2, 3])
    fig.savefig('myfig.png')
<
This prevents any interaction with GUI frameworks and the window manager.

It's alternatively still possible to use the pyplot interface: instead of
calling |matplotlib.pyplot.show|, call |matplotlib.pyplot.savefig|. In that
case, you must close the figure after saving it. Not closing the figure
causes a memory leak, because pyplot keeps references to all not-yet-shown
figures.

>python
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
    plt.plot([1, 2, 3])
    plt.savefig('myfig.png')
    plt.close()
<
  See also: ~

  Embed in a web application server (Flask)
  https://matplotlib.org/stable/gallery/user_interfaces/web_application_server_sgskip.html
  for information about running matplotlib inside of a web application.

WORK WITH THREADS                                *users-faq-work-with-threads*

*users-faq-how-to-threads*

Matplotlib is not thread-safe: in fact, there are known race conditions that
affect certain artists. Hence, if you work with threads, it is your
responsibility to set up the proper locks to serialize access to Matplotlib
artists.

You may be able to work on separate figures from separate threads. However,
you must in that case use a non-interactive backend (typically Agg), because
most GUI backends require being run from the main thread as well.

GET HELP                                                  *users-faq-get-help*

*users-faq-id1*

*users-faq-reporting-problems*

There are a number of good resources for getting help with Matplotlib. There
is a good chance your question has already been asked:

- The mailing list archive
  https://discourse.matplotlib.org/c/community/matplotlib-users/6.
- GitHub issues https://github.com/matplotlib/matplotlib/issues.
- Stackoverflow questions tagged matplotlib
  https://stackoverflow.com/questions/tagged/matplotlib.

If you are unable to find an answer to your question through search, please
provide the following information in your e-mail to the mailing list
https://mail.python.org/mailman/listinfo/matplotlib-users:

- Your operating system (Linux/Unix users: post the output of `uname -a`).
- Matplotlib version:

  >text
      python -c "import matplotlib; print(matplotlib.__version__)"
<

- Where you obtained Matplotlib (e.g., your Linux distribution's packages,
  GitHub, PyPI, or Anaconda https://www.anaconda.com/).
- Any customizations to your `matplotlibrc` file (see
  |users-explain-customizing-customizing|).
- If the problem is reproducible, please try to provide a minimal,
  standalone Python script that demonstrates the problem. This is the
  critical step. If you can't post a piece of code that we can run and
  reproduce your error, the chances of getting help are significantly
  diminished. Very often, the mere act of trying to minimize your code to
  the smallest bit that produces the error will help you find a bug in your
  code that is causing the problem.
- Matplotlib provides debugging information through the `logging`
  https://docs.python.org/3/library/logging.html#module-logging library, and
  a helper function to set the logging level: one can call

  >python
      plt.set_loglevel("INFO")  # or "DEBUG" for more info
<
  to obtain this debugging information.

  Standard functions from the `logging`
  https://docs.python.org/3/library/logging.html#module-logging module are
  also applicable; e.g. one could call `logging.basicConfig(level="DEBUG")`
  even before importing Matplotlib (this is in particular necessary to get
  the logging info emitted during Matplotlib's import), or attach a custom
  handler to the "matplotlib" logger. This may be useful if you use a custom
  logging configuration.

If you compiled Matplotlib yourself, please also provide:

- your compiler version -- e.g., `gcc --version`.
- the output of:

  >bash
      pip install --verbose
<
  The beginning of the build output contains lots of details about your
  platform that are useful for the Matplotlib developers to diagnose your
  problem.

If you compiled an older version of Matplotlib using the pre-Meson build
system, instead provide:

- any changes you have made to `setup.py`/`setupext.py`,
- the output of:

  >bash
      rm -rf build
      python setup.py build
<

Including this information in your first e-mail to the mailing list will
save a lot of time.

You will likely get a faster response writing to the mailing list than
filing a bug in the bug tracker. Most developers check the bug tracker only
periodically. If your problem has been determined to be a bug and cannot be
quickly solved, you may be asked to file a bug in the tracker so the issue
doesn't get lost.

    conda install -c conda-forge matplotlib

    pixi add matplotlib
<
uv: ~

>bash
    uv add matplotlib
<
  Warning: ~

  uv usually installs its own versions of Python from the
  python-build-standalone project, and only recent versions of those Python
  builds (August 2025) work properly with the `tkagg` backend for displaying
  plots in a window. Please make sure you are using uv 0.8.7 or newer
  (update with e.g. `uv self update`) and that your bundled Python installs
  are up to date (with `uv python upgrade --reinstall`). Alternatively, you
  can use one of the other supported GUI frameworks
  https://matplotlib.org/stable/install/dependencies.html#optional-dependencies,
  e.g.

  >bash
      uv add matplotlib pyside6
<

DRAW A FIRST PLOT              *users-getting_started-index-draw-a-first-plot*

Here is a minimal example plot:

>python
    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
    import numpy as np

    x = np.linspace(0, 2 * np.pi, 200)
    y = np.sin(x)

    fig, ax = plt.subplots()
    ax.plot(x, y)
    plt.show()
<
(`Source code`
https://matplotlib.org/stable/_downloads/33fdd7721ebf0a0381692a5848b85e3f/index-1.py,
`2x.png`
https://matplotlib.org/stable/_downloads/204d3ab65d82e7f8909502225bb5579e/index-1.2x.png,
`png`
https://matplotlib.org/stable/_downloads/643ceae5cb607472d92c85dde4ec5d08/index-1.png)

If a plot does not show up please check Troubleshooting
https://matplotlib.org/stable/devel/troubleshooting.html#troubleshooting-faq.

WHERE TO GO NEXT                *users-getting_started-index-where-to-go-next*

- Check out |matplotlib-plot_types-index| to get an overview of the types of
  plots you can create with Matplotlib.
- Learn Matplotlib from the ground up in the
  |users-explain-quick_start-quick-start|.

----------------------------------------------------------------------------
Glossary                                           *matplotlib-users-glossary*

This glossary defines concepts and terminology specific to Matplotlib.

Figure *users-glossary-term-Figure*
    The outermost container for a Matplotlib graphic. Think of this as the
    canvas to draw on.

    This is implemented in the class |matplotlib.figure.Figure|. For more
    details see |explain-figure-figure_intro-figure-intro|.

Axes *users-glossary-term-Axes*
    This is a container for what is often colloquially called a
    plot/chart/graph. It's a data area with |users-glossary-term-Axis|es,
    i.e. coordinate directions, and includes data artists like lines, bars
    etc. as well as decorations like title, axis labels, legend.

    Since most "plotting operations" are realized as methods on
    |matplotlib.axes.Axes| this is the object users will mostly interact
    with.

    Note: The term Axes was taken over from MATLAB. Think of this as a
    container spanned by the x- and y-axis, including decoration and data.

Axis *users-glossary-term-Axis*
    A direction with a scale. The scale defines the mapping from data
    coordinates to screen coordinates. The Axis also includes the ticks and
    axis label.

Artist *users-glossary-term-Artist*
    The base class for all graphical element that can be drawn. Examples are
    Lines, Rectangles, Text, Ticks, Legend, Axes, ...
